"He's lodging at a house where they make him eat his oats," he said. "He works two afternoons a week for a mender. He has a friend who's fifteen and very quick and tells him the truth about his feet." He watched her bind the pad on. "And he's learning things. He writes me every week. He's well."

All of it was true. None of it was the word. Alis had been there that morning on the corner, and had seen Pellin's face through the window, perhaps, or seen the boy's face coming out, and she knew it had gone badly. She did not know how badly, or what it had been called. She had not asked, and he did not tell her. He did not tell her about the reading room either, or the forty-eight hours, or the summons stamped *closed, pending*, or anything that had been in the letter now folded in the inside pocket of his coat. It was not his to tell. And she was kneeling on the floor of a healers' house in an apron too big for her, learning a trade, and she did not need to carry anything of his down Merchant Row tonight.

"Good," said Alis. She tied off the binding, and it was neat, neater than he could have done it with two good hands. "Tell him I carry basins." She almost smiled. "He'll laugh. He always said I'd be the one who ran a whole ward."

"He'll laugh," Hesk agreed.

She got up off the floor and took the basin away, and the senior healer came over afterward, as Alis had said she would, and looked at the binding and turned his hand in hers and said, "Who did this?" When Hesk said that the girl had, she grunted, and did not undo it, and went back to the carter.

He walked home up Merchant Row in the dusk with his bound hand in his coat pocket and the boy's letter against his chest. He found that he was less frightened than he had been on the step, though nothing had changed. A girl had knelt on a floor and washed a cut four times because three was not enough. He had sat there and watched her be careful, and it had steadied him, as the boy had once written that the sacks off Ressa's wagon steadied him at a kitchen table with a notice on it.

---

The review sat on the Friday of the week after, in the small room on the upper floor of the guild hall by the canal, with the shutters half closed against the light.

There were three of them at the table. One was the reviewing officer from the guild's own office, a thin careful man with a portfolio, whom Hesk had met once when he came to look at the workshop and ask about the drawer of springs. The other two were members, chosen by lot, as the guild's rules required. One was a lens-grinder from the Inner District whom Hesk knew only by sight. The other was a woman he had sat near at thirty years of guild suppers without ever once learning her name, which he realized only as he sat down. She was a gauge-maker with a shop on the canal, a small fierce woman with a magnifier on a ribbon round her neck. She looked at his bound hand and raised her eyebrows and said nothing.

The reviewing officer read out the notice. He read it in full, in the voice in which such things are read: Statute 14, Section 9; the residence; the standing; the paragraph that said what keeping the person close would cost. He read Hesk's own account after it, the one Hesk had written and sent and been asked for a copy of. *The person named in the registry notice of the second day no longer resides at this address. He left on the morning of the third day for another city, where he is lawfully resident, and he goes with my full support.*

"Is that still the case?" said the reviewing officer.

"It is."

"He has not returned to Denvash?"

"He has not."

"Do you maintain contact with him?"

Hesk had known this question would come, and had thought for a long time on several nights about how to answer it, and had decided in the end that there was only one answer he could give and still be able to look at his own hands afterward.

"I write to him every week," he said. "He writes to me. I intend to go on doing both."

The lens-grinder made a small note. The reviewing officer looked at him for a moment over the portfolio.

"The statute does not prohibit correspondence," he said at last, as though he were reading it off a page in his head. "It concerns residence and association. Correspondence is—" He seemed to choose a word. "Not specified."

"Then I'll go on with it," said Hesk, "until somebody specifies it."

The gauge-maker with the magnifier made a noise in her throat that might have been a cough.

They asked him about the workshop. They asked him about his work for the survey office and the canal company, and whether any of it was work in which the guild's good name could be said to rest on his. He told them it was levels and gauges, and that the young men at the survey office dropped them off ladders, and that the canal had not flooded anybody in thirty years on his figures. The lens-grinder nearly smiled. They asked whether he had made any application on the boy's behalf, to any office, for any exemption. He said he had not; there was none to make. They asked him whether he had anything to add.

He thought of a great deal he could add. He thought of a reading room under a man's floor, and a loose page tucked back in crooked, and four readings aloud. He thought of a girl he had never met who told the boy the truth about his feet. He thought of a level held for fifty minutes by a boy whose father said he was wasting his mornings.

"No," he said.

They sent him out to wait on the landing, on a hard chair, under a portrait of some guild master from before anybody now living had been born. He waited for most of an hour. He did not look at the portrait. He looked at his bound hand, and thought about the cut being washed four times.

When they called him back in, the reviewing officer did not sit down.

"The guild has considered the notice and the member's account," he said. "It finds no present cause to act on the member's standing." He closed the portfolio. "The matter will remain on the book."

"Remain," said Hesk.

"On the book. Open." The reviewing officer looked at him with something that was very nearly apology and was not allowed to be. "If the Compact writes to us again, about this, we will be obliged to look again. If it does not, the book will stay as it is."

So it was not closed. It was not lost, either. It was a door left on the latch, which was, Hesk thought, walking down the stairs with his good coat buttoned, very much the shape of everything else this autumn.

The gauge-maker caught him up at the bottom of the stairs. She did not say anything for a while. She walked beside him out into the street along the canal, with her magnifier swinging on its ribbon, until they came to the corner where she would go one way and he the other.

"The drawer of springs," she said. "He asked me about it, that man, when he came to my shop. Whether it was usual." She looked at his hand. "I told him every workshop in Denvash has a drawer of springs, and that a man who doesn't is either a liar or a bad maker." She snorted. "He wrote it down."

Then she went off along the canal toward her shop, and Hesk stood on the corner for some time, a little warmed, and still did not know her name.

---

He answered the boy's letter that night, at the kitchen table, with the lamp close and his bound hand stiff on the paper.

It was the hardest letter he had written since the first one. He knew what he wanted to say. It was choosing what to leave out that took him three sheets.

He put in the review. He put it in plainly: that it had sat, that they had asked whether he wrote to the boy and he had told them he did, every week, and meant to go on. He put in that they had found no present cause, and that the matter would remain on the book, and what *on the book* meant. He put in the gauge-maker and the drawer of springs, because the boy would like it.

He put in that he had read the citation three times and checked it as well as he could from Denvash with no handbook, and that it held, as far as he could see, as it had for the boy. He put in that he was proud of the reading, and of the four times aloud. That was the truest thing in the letter, and he left it short so it would stay true.

Then he came to the fear.

He sat for a long time with the pen lifted. He could leave it out. The boy had enough to carry without a letter from Denvash telling him that his grandfather had sat on a step in the street and been more frightened than at any time since Weaver's Row, a week after there was anything left to be frightened of. It would do no good. It could not reach back and change one thing that had happened at that desk upstairs at the post.

But they had made a bargain, the two of them, one letter apart. *I'd rather have the telling.* He had written that himself. *As it comes.* The boy had written that back.

He wrote it down.

*When your letter came, I sat on the workshop step in the street and read it there, and I was more afraid than I have been since the day at Weaver's Row. It was all over by then. You had already done it, and done it well, and I knew that by the second line. It made no difference. That's the post, and it's the price of where you are, and I'd pay it again. But you asked me once to tell you as it comes, and that's how it came.*

He did not put in the hand. He did not put in the spring. That was not leaving out what mattered. It was leaving out a broken spring in a drawer, which mattered to nobody but him and the survey office.

At the bottom, because he had promised, he wrote: *Joren says to tell you his heels are down. He held a level for me for fifty minutes without moving it. His father thinks he's wasting his mornings.*

And then, because it was also true: *Alis carries basins. She says you'll laugh.*

He signed it *— H.* He read it over once by the lamp, and found that it was all true, and that it was not all of what was true, and that he was not sure he had ever written a letter in his life of which both of those things could not be said.

---

"Bronze," said Lira.

She said it as if she were reading the word off the side of a crate, to see whether it was the crate she had ordered.

It was Tuesday morning, and Vell had her table out for the afternoon card, and they were standing in front of it because she had sent the pie boy to the low wall to fetch them. Cael was standing more or less straight, which had taken him two days to manage. The back was a band of colour from one shoulder blade to the other, going from black at the middle to green at the edges, and it pulled whenever he lifted his arms. The thigh had a print on it the width of a blade's flat, a long dark bar he could have laid a ruler along. His left forearm, over the old burn, had swollen up like a loaf and was going down slowly in cold water from the pump.

Lira had not let him do anything on Monday but sit on the low wall in the grey half and watch her drill. On Tuesday she had let him walk the box at the slowest speed she had. *Efficient,* she had said, when he argued. *I said I'd tell you. I'm telling you.*

"Bronze Rank 1," said Vell. "Blade Path. Her name's Sarel. She fights here a few times a season, when she's got nothing better on, and she's on Sunday because she asked for something on Sunday." She looked at Cael. "You'll have her if you want her. If you don't, I'll give her somebody else, and nobody will think the less of you. Most people in this yard wouldn't take her if I paid them."

"Why me?"

"Not to win." Vell turned her tea a quarter turn by its handle. "You've won three of seven in this book now, and two of them inside a week. Every one of them, you watched first, and found the thing, and the thing was there. I'd like to know what you do when it isn't. When there's nothing to find, or you can't find it fast enough to use." She looked at Lira. "She's quick. She's quicker than anything you've stood across from. And she's fair. She won't hurt you for the pleasure of it, and she won't make it easy for the pleasure of it either."

Lira was looking at Vell with her arms folded.

"Survivable?" she said.

It was not quite a question to Vell. It was the question Lira had promised to ask, on the first morning of the six weeks, by the post.

Vell considered her for a long moment, and seemed to understand exactly what it was.

"Survivable," she said. "If he's sensible. And if you've taught him to fall."

Lira looked at Cael. He looked back at her. He saw her weigh it, the way she weighed a bout between exchanges, quickly and completely. He saw her not like the answer she came to.

"He'll be sensible," she said. "I'll see to the falling."

Vell picked up her pen.

"She's on this afternoon, as it happens. Third bout," she said, writing. "And Thursday. Watch her both times." She did not look up. "I'd watch her with both eyes. I don't think it'll help you much. But I'd like to see what you write."

---

He watched Sarel fight that afternoon, at the rope on the shaded side, near the barrel, with the Log open on his knee and the pencil ready.

He did not write much.

She was about thirty, long-legged and lean, with dark hair cut short as a boy's and a long scar down the outside of one forearm, the left, old and white. She worked, somebody at the rope said, on the scaffolds the builders put up round the new houses on the east road. She had the scaffold walker's way of standing, with her weight easy and her arms loose, like somebody who has long since stopped thinking about how high she is. She came over the rope without any hurry at all. Her opponent was a Copper Rank 5, a Force man with a reputation, and he was not happy to be there.

"Begin when you're ready," said Vell.

Sarel's blade came up along her right forearm, pale and straight. It was no longer than Renn's, as far as Cael's eye could tell, and no brighter. Then she moved, and he understood why Vell had said what she had said.

She did not amble, or probe, or throw a figure from her mark. She simply arrived. She was on her mark, and then she was in front of the Force man, inside his reach, and the flat of her blade was already coming down on his shoulder. Cael had seen her start. He was sure he had seen her start. He had not seen her cross.

It was not like Lira's step. Lira's step folded the ground. This was only a body that was very fast, faster than any body he had seen, so fast that the watching could not keep up with it. By the time his eyes had told him *she's moving*, she had moved, and struck, and was already going somewhere else.

He wrote *closes very fast* and looked up, and she had done two more things while he wrote it.

He stopped writing. He watched. He watched her hips and her feet and her shoulders, all the places Hesk had taught him, and every place he looked she had already left. He tried to find the thing she did before she went. There had to be a thing; there was always a thing. The heel. The dip. The shimmer. The breath. The lean.

He could not find it. That was not because it was not there. It was because by the time he had looked for it in one place, she had done whatever she did, and he was looking for the next one.

The Force man went down in the third exchange, on his back, with the flat of her blade resting on his collarbone.

"Called," said Vell. "Blade Path."

Cael looked down at the Log. It said *closes very fast*, and under it *left foot?*, crossed out, and *shoulders?*, crossed out, and *something before she goes—*, unfinished.

Lira had come up beside him at some point and was standing very close, with her shoulder against his good one.

"Well?" she said.

"I couldn't write fast enough."

"No." She did not say anything else for a moment. "Nobody could. I couldn't, and I've spent half my life looking at fast people." She looked at the page. "That's the bout, Cael. That's what she's for. Not the page you couldn't fill." She took a breath. "You'll watch her on Thursday. You'll get one more line. Maybe two. And then on Sunday you'll go in with two lines against a woman who doesn't care about lines."

"And then?"

"And then you'll find out what you do when you've got nothing to look up." She bumped his shoulder very gently. "And I'll teach you to fall properly before then. Because you're going to."

On Thursday he watched Sarel again, against a Copper 4 Stone, and got one more line. *She doesn't look at you. She looks at the space next to you, where you're going to be.* He looked at the line for a long time on the low wall by the pump afterward, with the cold coming down. He knew it was true and he did not know what to do with it. Then he turned the page and wrote at the top of a clean one, *Sarel. Bronze Rank 1, Blade.* Under it there was nothing at all, and he left it like that, because nothing at all was the honest entry.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-01-the-shattered/manuscript/chapter-37.md

# Chapter 37 — Nothing to Look Up

"Again," said Lira. "And this time don't put your hand out."

He had put his hand out. He had put it out every time, eleven times that morning, because it was what a hand did when the ground came up at you, and every time she had stopped him and made him get up and do it again.

They were in the low corner of the yard by the drain, where the mist pooled in the mornings and the dirt was softest from the water that ran down off the public house roof. Lira had chosen it for that. She had said, with a perfectly straight face, that she wanted him to learn to fall on soft ground first so that he would have something to be disappointed about later.

"A hand out is a broken wrist," she said. "Or a broken arm, if she's heavy. You land on the meat. The side of your leg, your hip, your back across here." She slapped the back of her own shoulder. "Chin in. Roll. Let the ground have it. Don't argue with the ground. It's bigger than you."

"Is that a Wind saying?"

"It's a Lira saying. I've been knocked down more than anyone in this yard." She brought her hands up. "Again."

She came at him with the step, slowly, at half speed, and her shoulder came into his and took his feet. He went down sideways. He tucked his chin and let his hip take the dirt and rolled, and his hand stayed folded in against his chest, and he came up onto one knee with mud down his whole left side and his back screaming where the bruise was.

"There," said Lira. "That one."

She made a mark on the post with her chalk. It was not a *gone* mark. She had started a second column on the other side of the post for falls, and it had seven in it now, and the *gone* column had thirty-four.

He had stopped noticing the word. That was the strange thing. It was Saturday, the eighteenth morning since the board, and he still said it every time a plan died, as she had made him promise. But it came out of him now without any heat in his ears, and sometimes it came before he had properly decided the plan was dead. It came out, and he heard it, and his body was already doing the next thing while his mind caught up and agreed. It was beginning to feel less like a confession and more like a hinge.

They did the falls until the sun came over the east wall. Then Lira wanted the step at full speed, because she said she had been going slowly for him all week and her legs had forgotten what they were for.

She did it ten times. The two empty touches, high, quick, one from each hand. The ground folding. The backhand coming round at head height and stopping beside his ear. He got his counter half built on the fourth and on the seventh, which was better than one in ten. On the ninth she was breathing hard. She had been up since before light and had already done an hour at the post before he came, and he could see the morning in her shoulders.

On the tenth she went too far.

It was very small. The backhand came round and stopped by his ear, as it always did. But her weight did not stop with it. It went on past the place where the strike had ended, a hand's width further, onto her right foot, the way a door goes on swinging after you have let go of the handle. She had to put the right foot down hard and wide to catch herself. For half a breath she was standing open, with her whole right side turned toward him and nothing between his hands and her ribs.

Then she was back, and loose, and grinning, and pushing her hair off her face with her wrist.

"Again," she said. "No. Enough. Bread."

He had seen it once before. He had seen it in the fourth exchange against Brenna, three weeks ago, when she had gone in on Brenna's right and gone a hair further than she needed to. Brenna's cocked fist had come round and caught her on the side of the head as she came out. He had seen it at the rope, and he had decided at the rope that once was not a pattern, and he had left it out of her report.

That was twice.

He took the larger half of the bread and ate it and did not say anything.

He told himself that he would tell her after Sunday. He told himself that she should not be carrying a thing about her own feet into a week when she would be at the barrel watching him get hit by a Bronze. That was true. It was the kind of true that has its legs sawn short, and he knew it, and he ate his bread.

---

The yard was full on Sunday by the middle of the afternoon, and it was not full for him.

He understood that the moment he came through the gate in the wall. The benches were out and the third row was already crowded with old men in scarves. The rope was three deep on the shaded side. But the noise of the crowd was a different noise from the one there had been for Renn. It was not the noise of people who had come to see what would happen. It was the noise of people who had come to see something they already knew would happen, done well.

They had come for Sarel. She fought here a few times a season, Vell had said, when she had nothing better on. When she did, people came.

Marrow's slate said *Sarel* with no price beside it at all. Under it, *unrated (vouched)*, with a price so long it ran off the edge of the slate and had been chalked round the corner. And under that, *Over/under: 3 exch.*

"Three," said Lira at the barrel. "He thinks you'll last three. That's generous."

"Is it?"

"It's Marrow. It's whatever makes him money." She had her arms folded on the rope and her eyes on the far side of the circle, where Sarel was standing by the water bucket talking to the woman who sold the water, easy, with one hand in her pocket. "Remember the falling."

"I remember the falling."

"Chin in. Land on the meat. Don't argue with the ground." She was not looking at him. "And remember what it's for. It's not for winning. Vell said so and she's right. You find out what you do when there's nothing to look up. That's all."

"And if I don't find anything?"

Lira turned her head and looked at him at last.

"Then you'll have found that," she said. "That's still something. Go on."

The first bout was two young Blades, and it was quick, and nobody remembered it.

Then Vell stood up.

"Second bout." She did not need to wait for the yard to go quiet. It had been quiet since she stood. "Sarel. Bronze Rank 1, Blade Path. Against unrated. Vouched." The small pause. "Begin when you're ready."

Sarel came over the rope.

She came over without hurry, as she had on Tuesday and Thursday. She was taller than he had thought from the rope, long in the leg and lean all through, and the scaffold walker's ease was in everything she did. She walked to her mark and stood on it. She did not look at him. He saw her not look at him. She looked at the dirt just beside his left foot, and then at the dirt beside his right, as though she were measuring a floor for a carpet.

The blade came up along her right forearm, pale and straight.

"Begin," said Vell.

He watched her hips.

That was the first thing, and it was a mistake, and he knew it was a mistake even as he made it. He had decided on Thursday night that watching would not work and that he would watch anyway for one exchange, because he had to know, and because he did not have anything else. So he stood on his mark with his hands up and his eyes on her hips, and he waited for the thing she did before she went.

She went.

He saw her start. He was sure of that afterward. He saw her weight go forward, and he had time to think *now*, and to begin to move. Then she was in front of him, inside his reach, and the flat of her blade was coming down across his right shoulder. His mind arrived at that fact a long way behind his body. It landed like a plank dropped from a height, a broad flat slap that spun him and took his feet.

He fell.

He fell the way Lira had taught him for four mornings. He did not put his hand out. He tucked his chin and let his right hip have the ground, and rolled, and came up on one knee with dirt down his whole side. Sarel was already walking back to her mark.

Along the rope somebody laughed, not unkindly.

*First exchange,* he said to himself, in Lira's report voice. *She started and I saw her start. I didn't see her cross. I fell properly.*

That was all there was to report, so he got up.

In the second exchange he tried his one line.

*She doesn't look at you. She looks at the space next to you, where you're going to be.* He had written it on Thursday and looked at it for an hour. It meant that if he went where he would naturally go, she would be there first. So when she came, he would not go where he would naturally go. He would go the other way.

"Begin."

She looked at the dirt beside his left foot. She went. He saw her go, and he went the other way, right, away from where she was looking, as fast as he had ever moved.

She was there.

She had not been looking at the left at all, or she had, and had seen him begin to go right, and had simply gone right as well, faster. He did not know which. He would never know which. The flat came across his ribs on the right side, low, where Renn had put it on the first night, and it lifted him and put him down. He landed on his back across the groove with every bit of breath gone.

He lay there for a moment looking at the sky, which was white and very far away. He had fallen properly, he thought. Chin in. On the meat. He had not argued with the ground.

He got up. It took longer.

He stood on his mark with his ribs on fire and his shoulder throbbing, and he understood something, all at once and quite clearly. It was not the comfortable clarity of a figure coming right. It was the other kind, the kind that comes when you have pushed hard on a bracket and felt it give.

Everything he did was watching. It was the whole of him. He watched, and found the thing, and planned from the thing. It had beaten Dessa and the kiln boy and Renn, and it had kept him standing against everyone else. It was the oldest thing he owned, older than the Log, older than Ardenmere. Hesk had given it to him on a Tuesday in a community hall in Denvash. *The third exchange is where they tell you the truth.*

Sarel was not going to tell him the truth in the third exchange. She was going to tell it in the first, and the second, and every one after it. She was telling it now, and it was simply too fast to read. By the time he had read anything, it was already over.

The watching was too slow for her. Not wrong. Too slow.

"Gone," said Cael, standing on his mark.

He did not say it loudly. But the yard had gone quiet between the exchanges, the way it did for a Bronze, and he heard it carry. Along the rope, on the shaded side, Lira's head came up. Across the circle, for the first time, Sarel looked at him and not at the dirt beside him. Her eyebrows went up a very little.

"Begin," said Vell.

He did not watch her.

He did not know what else to do, so he did what Lira did. It was the thing she had been trying to teach him for two months and that he had nodded at and gone on not doing, morning after morning. He moved. He did not move to anywhere. He did not decide where he would be and then go there. He moved small and quick and all the time, a half step left, a quarter step back, a shift of the weight, a turn of the hip. He did not plan any of it. If he had stopped to plan it, it would have been a plan, and she would have read it in the space next to him before he had finished.

He did not know where he would be in half a breath. That was the whole point. If he did not know, there was nothing in the space next to him for her to read.

She came. He was somewhere else.

Not far. A hand's width, perhaps, no more. The flat of her blade went past his left ear with a hiss, close enough for him to feel the air of it on his cheek. He had not seen it coming. He had not dodged it. He had simply not been where it was going, because he had not known where he was going himself.

She came again, from the other side. He was somewhere else again. The edge of the flat brushed his sleeve, and he felt it, and it did not land.

The third time she did not come. She stood a pace off, with her blade out, and looked at him.

The yard had gone very quiet. Along the rope, in the third row of the benches, an old man said *huh*, out loud, to nobody.

"Called," said Vell. "End of the exchange. Nothing."

He went back to his mark. His legs were trembling. He had moved more in that one exchange than in the two before it together, and his body did not know how to do it. It was like trying to write with the wrong hand. Every step was a step he had to find, and he found them late, and badly. But they had been his, and she had not been able to read them, because there had been nothing in them to read.

*Third exchange,* he said to himself. *I stopped watching. I didn't plan. She didn't land.*

He looked across the circle. Sarel was standing on her mark with her blade out and her head a little on one side. She was looking at him the way Renn had looked at him in the third exchange of the first night. It was a narrow, careful look, the look of somebody who has found a thing on her bench that she did not put there.

Then she nodded, once, very slightly, to herself. It was not his nod. It was the nod of somebody who has solved something.

*She's learned it,* he thought. *She's already learned it.*

"Begin."

She came in, and she did not come in to strike.

She came in and stayed. She came close, very close, inside the reach of her own blade, and she did not try to land anything. She simply moved with him. When he took his half step left, she took hers. When he shifted back, she was there. She did not need to read where he was going. She only had to keep the space around him small, so small that there was nowhere for him to go that she was not already standing next to. She walked him like that, close, round the inside of the groove. She was so near that he could hear her breathing, which was easy, and smell the lime dust from the scaffolds on her shirt, and see the old white scar down her left forearm. She walked him, a step at a time, toward the long side of the circle, where the drain was. He felt the groove come up under his heel.

He had nowhere left to go but down.

He went down. He did not fall. He chose it. He dropped low and rolled away sideways along the groove, chin in, on the meat. It was the first time he had ever used a fall for anything but surviving one. He came up a stride and a half away, on one knee, with the flat of her blade arriving late across his left forearm as he rose. It landed on the old burn, on Renn's swelling, which had only just gone down.

The white came up behind his eyes. He did not let go of anything.

"Called," said Vell. "End of the exchange."

He got to his mark. He did not remember walking there.

*Fourth exchange. She learned it. She stopped trying to find me and made the place small. I went down on purpose.* He stopped. *My arm.*

The arm was bad. He could feel the fingers of his left hand, but he could not make them close. He held it against his chest, and breathed, and looked across at Sarel. She was looking at the arm, and then at his face, and her face did not do anything at all.

In the fifth he tried to hit her.

He did not plan that either. He had only one strike he owned, short and straight from the hip, and he had decided that if she came close again, as she had in the fourth, he would throw it. He would not choose a time. He would throw it the moment she was there, at whatever was in front of him, without stopping to see whether it was a good idea.

She came close. He threw it.

It was slow. He felt how slow it was as it went, the back foot driving and the shoulder following and the fist arriving last, every piece of it in the right order and every piece of it a breath behind her. She was not there when the fist arrived. But she had been there when it started, and his knuckles brushed the cloth of her sleeve on the way past. It was only cloth, the barest touch, the way a moth touches a lamp.

The rope heard it before he did. It made a sound, a short sharp intake all along the shaded side, as though forty people had all burned their fingers at once.

Then her flat came across his back, across the bruise Renn had left, from one shoulder blade to the other. He went down on his hands and stayed there for a moment, looking at the dirt between them, and could not find his breath anywhere.

He found it.

"Called," said Vell. "End of the exchange."

He got up. It took three tries. Lira was standing at the barrel with both hands on the rope, very white, and she was not biting her knuckle. She was saying something. He could see her mouth move. He could not hear it over the noise in his own ears.

He stood on his mark for the sixth exchange, and he knew it was the last, and he knew why. His legs had gone. They had gone in the fifth, somewhere between the strike and the ground, and they were not coming back today. He could still do the small quick moving. He could feel how to do it now. It was there in him, new and clumsy, the way a word is there in a language you have just started to learn. But he could do it only as fast as his legs would let him, and they would hardly let him at all.

"Begin."

Sarel came.

He moved. He moved small and quick and unplanned, as he had in the third, and for one breath, for two, he was somewhere else, and she was not there. On the third breath his legs were a half-step slow. It was a very small half step. It was all she needed.

The flat of her blade came across his ribs on the right side, exactly where she had put it in the second exchange. He was on his back across the groove before he had understood that he had been hit. The blade came down and rested on his collarbone, lightly, the way you would lay a ruler on a page.

"Called," said Vell. "Blade Path."

---

He lay on his back and looked at the white sky and breathed. It was the only thing he could think of to do.

The blade went out along Sarel's arm. Then she did a thing that he did not expect, and that he heard the rope not expect. She crouched down beside him in the dirt, low on her heels, with her forearms across her knees, the way the scaffold people sat to eat their lunch. Her face was level with his.

She looked at him for a while. She did not seem to be in any hurry about it. Up close she was older than he had thought, with lines at the corners of her eyes from the sun on the scaffolds, and a nick out of one ear.

"How old are you?" she said.

"Fourteen."

She nodded, as if that were a number she had suspected and wanted to have confirmed.

"Two," she said.

He did not understand.

"Two exchanges." She held up two fingers. "You tried the thing you know, twice, and it didn't work, and in the third you threw it away." She put the hand back on her knee. "You know what most people do, at your rating, against somebody quicker? They watch harder. They watch the whole bout. They're still watching when they're on their backs. They go home and tell everybody they nearly had it. Six exchanges of watching harder." She shook her head. "You quit in two. You said it out loud. I heard you."

"It wasn't enough. The other thing."

"No," she agreed. "It wasn't. It was the first time you'd ever done it. You can tell." She looked at his left arm, which he was holding on his chest. "It'll be better the tenth time. Or the hundredth. I don't know which. I'm not your teacher." She glanced toward the barrel, where Lira was. "She is, I'd guess."

"Yes."

"Good. She moves like that. You'll want to steal it off her." Sarel stood up, all in one easy movement, the way she did everything. She did not hold out her hand to pull him up. She looked down at him for a moment longer from her full height, and the look was plain and direct and not at all soft.

"That was a good bout," she said. "Don't ask me for another one. You'll want to. Don't."

She went to the table. Cael lay on the dirt a moment longer, because he could. Then he rolled onto his side, chin in, and got his knees under him, and got up.

---

Vell wrote for a long time.

He stood in front of the table and waited, as you waited, with his left arm against his chest and his ribs pulling at every breath. The pen went down one line, and then another, and another. It stopped. It went on. She did not look up.

"Result stands as called," she said at last. "Sarel, Bronze Rank 1, Blade Path, over unrated. Sixth exchange. Called." The pen moved once more. "Witnessed and entered."

Then she looked up.

"Six," said Vell.

It was what she had said after Amrit, and after Petra, every time one more. It was not the same, and he heard that it was not the same. She did not explain it. She went back to her book.

Lira was waiting at the barrel. She did not say anything. She held out his coat, and when he could not get his left arm into the sleeve she put the coat round his shoulders instead. They walked out through the gate in the wall together, slowly, with her on his right side, the rib side, without being asked.

She did not ask for a report until they were two streets away.

"What happened in the second?" she said. "Between the second and the third. On your mark. You said it."

"I said gone."

"I know what you said. I heard it at the barrel. Half the rope heard it." She was looking straight ahead. "What was gone?"

He walked for a while before he answered, because it was hard to say and his ribs did not like it.

"The watching," he said. "All of it. The whole method. Everything I do." He looked at the cobbles. "It's too slow for her. It's not wrong. It's just too slow. By the time I've seen anything, it's over."

Lira did not say anything for a long time.

"And the third?"

"I did what you do."

She stopped walking.

They were at the corner by the chalk board at the bottom of the yard lane, under a lantern somebody had just lit, and she stood there in its light and looked at him. Her face did something he had not seen it do before. It was not the morning brightness, or the quick smile, or the slow one. It was nearer to the look of somebody who has been shouting into a well for two months and has just heard the bottom answer.

"Badly," he said. "I did it badly."

"I know you did it badly. I watched every step of it." Her voice was not steady. "You did it, though."

"For one exchange."

"For one exchange," said Lira. "Against a Bronze. On your first try." She started walking again, quickly, so that he had to hurry, which hurt. "You're an idiot. Tomorrow you're doing nothing at all. Nothing. And the day after, we start on it properly."

---

He wrote it up that night on his cot, by the light of Yeni's lamp, because he could not sit on the low wall by the pump. He could not sit on anything hard. He wrote it at greater length than any entry in the Log, longer than Dessa, longer than either Renn.

*Ninth. Sarel, Bronze Rank 1, Blade. Lost, sixth exchange, called.*

*Claim: I can't find her tell. Evidence: two bouts watched, both eyes. A page with three crossings-out on it. Ruling: there probably is one. I'm too slow to see it.*

*Claim: watching is too slow for her. Evidence: first exchange, I saw her start and didn't see her cross. Second exchange, I went against my own habit and she was there anyway. Ruling: the method is fine against people slower than my eyes. She isn't slower than my eyes.*

*Third exchange, I stopped watching and moved without deciding. She didn't land. Fourth, she learned it and made the space small. Fifth, I touched her sleeve. Sixth, my legs went.*

He stopped there for a long time with the pencil.

*I need a second way,* he wrote. *For people I can't out-watch. Something that doesn't go through my eyes first. Lira has it. She doesn't decide and then move. She moves and the deciding is in it, somehow, all at once. I did a little of it today, badly, for one exchange.*

*I don't know if I can learn it from watching her. That's how I learn everything. But this might be the one thing watching can't teach, because it's the thing that isn't watching.*

*I don't know. Write that down: I don't know.*

He read it back. It was true all the way through. He turned the book over to the back and sat with it open, and he thought about the second way, and about Lira in the grey half, moving without deciding. He thought about the backhand going on past the place it should have stopped, on her right side, when she was tired. He thought about a report given in the dusk with a thing left out of it, and a morning, yesterday, when he had seen it again and eaten his bread.

He did not write any of that down. He told himself he was too tired. He blew out his small piece of the light by turning his face to the wall, and lay a long time with his ribs pulling and his arm on fire, and did not sleep for a while.

---

Lira made him do nothing on Monday, as she had said. He went to the mender in the afternoon and sorted washers one-handed, and the mender looked at his left arm and said nothing at all.

On Tuesday he could lift the arm to his shoulder. On Wednesday morning, in the grey half, she made him stand by the post and watch while she showed him what he had looked like against Sarel.

She did the first exchange. She stood on her mark with her feet too square and her hands up and her eyes very wide, fixed on an imaginary pair of hips, and she nodded to herself. The girls on the wall laughed so hard that one of them nearly went off it backward.

"You nodded," said Lira. "At a Bronze. You stood there and agreed with yourself, and she was already halfway across."

"I know."

"I know you know. I want you to see it." She did the second exchange. She was him going right, very fast, away from where she was looking, with his chin up and a look on his face of tremendous cleverness. Then she was him being hit, and she flung herself down sideways in the dirt so exactly the way he had gone down that he felt the bruise on his ribs throb in sympathy. The girls on the wall applauded.

She got up, filthy, and grinning.

"Now the third," she said, and stopped grinning. "The third I'll do properly. Because you did it properly, mostly, and you need to see what it looks like from out here."

She did the third.

She did his version of her moving, the small quick unplanned half steps. She did it badly on purpose, a little stiff, a little late, exactly as he had done it. Then she did it again, her own way, loose and quick, and the difference between the two was as plain as the difference between a hinge that swung true and one that caught at the halfway. Then she did it a third time, his way and her way at once. He could not have explained how she did it. He saw at last where his stiffness was. It was in the hips. He was moving his feet and keeping his hips square, as if they still had to be ready for a plan.

"There," he said.

"There." She stopped, breathing. "You see it?"

"I see it."

"Good." She put her hands on her knees for a moment. She had been going for most of an hour already, his way and her way and his way again, flinging herself in the dirt, and she was tired. He saw it in her shoulders. "One more. Then bread."

She did the step, for the end of it, as she always did. Two empty touches. The ground folding. The backhand coming round.

And her weight went on past it, onto her right foot, a hand's width too far. She caught herself with the foot down hard and wide, and for half a breath her whole right side was open to him, and then she was back.

Three times.

He did not let himself eat any bread first.

"Lira," he said.

She was already turning toward the post for it. She looked round.

"When you're tired," he said, "you go too far on your right. After the strike. You don't stop where the backhand stops. You go on past it, onto your right foot, a hand's width, maybe more. You have to put the foot down to catch yourself." He made himself keep going. "For half a breath your whole right side is open. Anybody quick could have it."

She stood very still by the post.

"Show me," she said.

He showed her. He did it the way she did it to him, slowly, from outside. He did the touches and the step, badly, and then the backhand, and he let his weight go on past the end of it, onto his right foot, too far. He caught himself with the foot down wide and stood there open, exactly the shape she had been a moment ago.

Lira looked at him standing there. She looked for a long time.

"I lost a bout like that," she said.

She said it quietly, and not to him.

"At the academy. At the end of a season, against a girl who wasn't as good as me. She wasn't. Everybody knew she wasn't." She was looking at the dirt. "It went long. Seven exchanges. In the seventh I went in on her right and she caught me coming out, on the side of the head, and I went down. And I lay there and I didn't know why. I went over it for weeks. I went over every step, and I couldn't find it, and in the end I decided she'd been lucky." Her voice went odd. "She wasn't lucky. Was she? I was tired. And I went too far on my right."

"I don't know. I wasn't there."

"No. But I was." She looked up at him, and her face was doing two things at once, and he watched them both. One was a kind of startled gladness, very bright, like somebody who has found a coin they lost a year ago in a coat they had stopped wearing. "Cael. That's—" She laughed, a short shaky sound. "Nobody's ever—that's *mine*. That's a thing about *me*. All this time I've been walking round with it, and nobody saw it. My instructor didn't see it. I didn't see it." She put her hand flat on her own right hip, as if to feel where it lived. "That's useful. That's really, actually useful. That's the most useful thing anybody's ever told me about my own feet."

He had known she would be glad. He had not known she would be glad like that.

"When I'm tired," said Lira, half to herself, already turning it over. "It's when I'm tired. All right. All right. I can work with tired." Then she looked up again. He saw the other thing come into her face, the second one, slower, the one that had been waiting under the first.

"You said *three*," she said. "Just now. Under your breath. When I did it."

He had not known he had said it out loud.

"How many times have you seen it?"

He thought about lying. He thought about it for the length of one breath, standing in the grey half with the chalk marks on the post beside him. There were thirty-odd *gone*s on one side and seven falls on the other, every one of them a thing he had done wrong and said out loud. He did not lie.

"Three. Saturday. Just now." He made himself finish it. "And once before that. At Brenna. The fourth exchange. You went in on her right and went too far, and she caught you on the side of the head."

Lira was very still.

"That was three weeks ago."

"Yes."

"You gave me a report." Her voice was quite level. "That night. In the lane. You gave me every exchange. You gave me the old men in the third row liking it." She was looking at him now, straight at him, with nothing in her face at all. "You didn't give me that."

"Once isn't a pattern," he said. "I didn't know if it was anything. I thought—"

"You *ruled*." She said it quietly. It was worse than if she had shouted. "I said no ruling. I said it twice. *Give it me after, like a report, I don't want a ruling.* And you stood at that rope and saw a thing about my fighting and decided it didn't count yet, and kept it." She took a breath. "Where did you put it? In your book. Did you write it down?"

He did not answer. He did not have to.

"In the back," said Lira. "You put my fight in the back of your book."

"I didn't write it anywhere. I kept it." He heard how that sounded. "I was going to tell you when I was sure."

"When you were sure." She said it after him, slowly, as if she were reading it off something. "That's what you do, isn't it. You keep it till you're sure. You keep everything till you're sure." She looked down at the post, and at the chalk, and back at him. "And Saturday. You saw it Saturday. That was twice. Twice is a pattern, in your book. I've heard you say it. And you ate your bread."

"I didn't want you carrying it into Sunday."

"That was my choice." Her voice went up, just a little, and she brought it down again at once. "It's my feet. My right side. My bout that I lost at the academy and never knew why. You don't get to decide when I'm ready to know about my own feet, Cael. Nobody does. Somebody decided things like that for me once, at a desk, and that's how I ended up on a wall in this district." She stopped.

He stood there in the grey half with nothing to say. It was all true. He had kept it until he was sure, and he had been sure on Saturday, and he had eaten his bread.

Lira looked at him a moment longer. Then she picked up the bread off the top of the post. She did not tear it. She held it in her hand as if she had forgotten what it was.

"Thank you for telling me," she said. "I mean that. It's the best thing you've ever given me, and I'm glad of it, and I'm going to use it." She put the bread down on the post again, whole. "I'm going now. I'll see you."

She did not say *same time tomorrow*.

She went out through the gate in the wall without looking back. He stood by the post in the grey half, with the bread beside him and the chalk marks under his hand, and listened to her quick feet go away down the lane until he could not hear them.

The girls on the wall had stopped laughing some time ago. One of them looked at him, and then looked away, as if she had seen something she was not supposed to.

He did not eat the bread. He stood there until the sun came over the east wall and there was no grey half left. He thought about a boy at a kitchen table in Denvash, two months and a lifetime ago, finding out that his grandfather had known for a few years. He thought about what his own face had done when he heard it.

He thought about four things on a page in the back of the Log that Hesk did not know.

Then he picked up the bread and went to the mender's, because it was Wednesday, and he did not know what else to do.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-01-the-shattered/manuscript/chapter-38.md

# Chapter 38 — A Door

Lira got as far as the board before she had to stop walking.

She had meant to go straight down the lane and through the triangle and on, anywhere, at the pace she used for markets, so that people got out of her way without knowing why. Her feet had other ideas. They took her down the lane quickly enough. Then at the board they simply stopped, as if somebody had put a hand on her chest, and she stood in front of it in the cold with her hands in her jacket pockets and pretended to read it.

There was a new card for Sunday in Vell's upright pencil, on the back of a bill for candles. There was the result from last Sunday, already going pale: *Sarel (Br 1, Blade) over unrated (vouched), sixth exchange, called*. Somebody had drawn a small circle round *sixth* in charcoal. Under that was the Renn result, and under that, nearly gone into the grain, her own. *Lira (Cu 3, Wind) over Brenna (Cu 2, Shield), fifth exchange, called.* The three charcoal strokes beside it had been rubbed half away by a sleeve.

She looked at her own name for a while.

She was angry. That was the first thing, and it was the biggest, and she let it be big because she had learned a long time ago that if you tried to make anger smaller while you were still standing in it, it only went somewhere you could not see it and waited. So she stood in front of the board and was angry, properly, all the way through, at a boy with a notebook who had stood at the rope two weeks ago, seen a thing about her, and decided she could not have it yet.

*Once isn't a pattern.*

She knew exactly what his face had looked like when he decided that. She could see it as clearly as if she had been standing beside him. He would have had his chin tucked and his eyes very wide, and he would have given the small private nod he did not know he did. *That's settled. Put it somewhere. Not now.* She had watched him make that face a hundred times about other people's feet. She had never once thought he would make it about hers.

And the worst of it was that she would have done the same. That was the thing she did not want to look at, standing in front of the board with her own name going pale on it. If it had been the other way round, and she had seen a thing in his fighting once, in a bout, she might not have said so either. She might have waited to see it again. She would have called it being careful.

But she would not have left it out of a *report*. Not after he had asked her for no ruling. Not after he had given her every other exchange, so carefully, in the dusk, with the old men in the third row put in for evidence. That was what she could not get past. He had made the report into something it was not. It had looked like everything he saw, and it had been everything he saw except one thing, and he had decided which thing.

Somebody had decided things for her once. A woman at a desk had decided that a thing Lira had done in front of witnesses had not counted, because it was not in the book with the diagrams. Lira had stood in front of that desk with her chin up and said *what was the exam actually testing*, and the woman had not answered, and that was the end of the academy. She had not thought about that desk for a long time. She was thinking about it now, and she did not like that a boy who was the opposite of that desk in every way had put her in mind of it.

A cart went by behind her. The skewer woman was setting up at the corner of the widest street, and she looked across at Lira standing still in front of the board, which Lira never did. She looked for a moment and then looked away, which was kind of her.

Lira turned her back on the board and walked on.

The other thing came up under the anger as she walked, slowly, the way the warmth comes up in your feet after you have been standing in snow. She had not let it come up in the yard because she had not wanted him to see it. She let it now.

*When you're tired, you go too far on your right.*

She went over it as she went down through the triangle. She went over the backhand, and the weight going on past the end of it, and her right foot coming down hard and wide to catch her. She had felt that foot come down a thousand times. She had never once thought about it. It was simply what her right foot did at the end of a long morning, like her hair coming loose on one side. You did not think about your hair coming loose. It came loose.

And then the academy. The seventh exchange against a girl who was not as good as her. Lira had gone over that bout for weeks, lying on her bed in the long dormitory with her eyes open, trying every step of it from every side, and she had never found it. She had decided in the end that the girl had been lucky. It was not a good answer. It was the only one left.

It had been her right foot. It had been a hand's width. It had been there the whole time, in plain sight, at the end of every long morning, and nobody had seen it. Her instructor had not. The woman at the desk had not. She had not.

A boy from Denvash had seen it, from a rope, on a cold afternoon, while his own heart was going so hard against his ribs that he had printed the rope into his palms.

She found that she had stopped again, in the middle of the street, and that she was smiling, and that she was still angry, and that both were true at once. A man with a barrow had to go round her. He said something. She did not hear what.

It was the strangest feeling she had ever had about another person, and she stood in the street and made herself look at it straight, because she did not believe in looking at things sideways. Somebody had looked at her and seen her exactly, all the way down to her feet. Nobody had ever done that before, not once, not anybody, not in her whole life. And then he had kept back the one thing he saw that she most needed to know.

Both of those were him. That was the trouble. You could not have the first one without the kind of person who did the second.

She thought, then, because she was honest with herself when nobody was looking, about a row of three things she kept in her own head.

The first was a hand at the board on the first day that had moved before it should have. The second was the third exchange against Renn on the first night. The third was a boy dropping under her backhand in the grey half, lower than she had ever asked him to go, and coming up standing in a way that was not his. She had never told him about the row. And she had never told him that she had gone into the yard alone, three mornings running, before he came, and tried the drop eleven times. She had learned that it was easy to do and impossible to do in time.

She had kept that. She had kept it for a month. She had told herself she was waiting until he had something he wanted to tell her.

It did not make what he had done all right. It took some of the heat out, that was all, the way a bucket of water takes some of the heat out of a forge without putting it out.

She walked on, and she did not go anywhere she had meant to go. She went where her feet had been going all along, two streets over from Torvin's, to a narrow lane she had walked down nine times in the last month on purpose and pretended each time that it was on the way to somewhere else.

---

The chandler's shop was at the end of the lane, with its door open and a smell of tallow coming out of it, and candles hanging in bunches from the beam over the counter by their wicks, like onions.

The widow who kept it was sitting behind the counter dipping tapers. She was a small woman with a face as round and pale as one of her own candle ends, and she had told Lira, the first time Lira came in to ask about the room upstairs, that she did not let to fighters. Then she had looked at Lira's boots, which had been resoled twice, and her jacket cuffs, which were turned back and stitched. And she had said that she supposed fighters had to sleep somewhere, and that she would want two weeks paid first and no visitors after the lamps.

"I'll hold it till Wednesday," she had said, a week ago. "Then I'll let it to whoever asks."

It was Wednesday.

"I've come about the room," said Lira.

The widow looked up from her tapers.

"I said Wednesday," she said, "and you've come on Wednesday." She seemed faintly surprised by this, as if people seldom did. "Two weeks first."

Lira took the tin out of the inside of her jacket.

It was a small flat tin that had once held throat sweets, and the picture on its lid had worn away to a brown smudge. She had carried it ever since she came to Ardenmere, in the inside pocket where it pressed against her ribs when she sat down. She opened it on the counter between the bunches of candles. Inside were the weeks of door, in small worn coins: the purse from Brenna, and the two summer purses before that, and every coin from every week she had eaten at Amrit's for half price because he had decided she was his. There was the money from the sparring hour across the district that she did not tell anybody about. There were three coins she had found in the groove at the Cinder House since the summer, dropped by people at the rope, and had kept because they felt like the yard's own.

She counted out two weeks.

She counted it twice, the way Torvin did, not because she did not trust the coins but to check her own attention. Then she pushed it across the counter. There was not very much left in the tin afterward. She shut the lid on what there was.

The widow counted it once, quickly, and swept it into a drawer, and took a key off a nail behind her.

"Stair's round the side," she said. "Mind the fourth step. It talks."

---

The room was at the top of a stair so narrow and steep that Lira had to go up it with one hand on the wall, and the fourth step did talk, a long creak like an old man complaining.

There was a door at the top.

It was a plain door, made of four boards with a crosspiece, painted a long time ago in a green that had gone grey. It had a latch, and a keyhole below the latch, and a scuff at the bottom where somebody had kicked it shut for years. She stood on the top step with the key in her hand and looked at it for a long time.

Then she put the key in and turned it. The lock was stiff, and then it was not. She pushed the door open and went in, and shut it behind her, and stood with her back against it.

The room was very small. It was smaller than the roof room in the tall house in the third lane, and she had shared that with two sisters from the dye sheds who talked in their sleep about indigo. There was a bed frame with a straw mattress rolled up on it, and a hook on the back of the door, and a shelf. There was a window, one, looking down into the lane, with a crack across one pane that somebody had stuck over with a strip of paper. The ceiling sloped down on the window side so that she would have to remember not to stand up too fast in the morning. It smelled faintly of tallow from the shop below. The floorboards were worn pale down the middle where somebody had walked from the door to the window, and from the window to the door, a great many times.

She stood there with her back against the door and listened to it be shut.

That was all she had wanted. She had not been able to say it to anybody, not to Yeni, who knew everything anyway, and not to Cael. When he asked what she was saving for, all she had been able to say was *a door*, and then tell him not to make a face about it. It had sounded like nothing. It was nothing. It was four boards and a crosspiece and a lock that stuck. But it was hers, and it shut, and when it was shut there was nobody on the other side of it who had any right to open it.

She had not had that since she left the academy. She had not had it at the academy either, in the long dormitory with its rows of beds. And before the academy—

She did not think about before the academy. She had a rule about that, and she kept it, the way you keep a door shut in a house you have moved out of.

She sat down on the floor, in the middle of the pale worn strip, with her back against the bed frame and her knees drawn up. She sat there for a long time, in the thin light from the cracked window, and let herself think about the six weeks. It was the first time she had sat still long enough to do it.

They were half gone. Twenty-two days of them were gone, and twenty were left. She had counted the days without meaning to, the way he would have.

In twenty-two days he had fought four times. He had beaten a boy who held his breath, and Renn, and nearly nobody had believed it about Renn until they saw it. He had lost to a Bronze in the sixth exchange, and in the third he had done the thing she had been trying to teach him for two months. He had done it badly, stiff in the hips. But he had done it, in front of the whole yard, against a woman the whole yard was afraid of. He had said *gone* thirty-four times at her post with his ears going red. Then he had said it once more at a Bronze with his ears not going red at all, and half the rope had heard him.

She had watched every one of those. She had stood at the barrel with her hands on the rope and watched him get better. It was not the slow, careful getting better of the first weeks, when every morning bought a hand's width and cost a bruise. It was faster now. He had found something, or it had found him, and he was coming up through the circle the way she had said people came up who went down faster, and he was not going down. He was being careful. She had made him careful.

She thought about being his teacher. She had never been anybody's teacher before. She had been somebody's student and failed, and somebody else's student and been right in front of witnesses, and had walked the yards of this district on her own for months, losing. She had not known, when she put her name to a vouch for a boy from Denvash who said a word nobody had ever heard out loud, that it would feel like this. She had not known it would feel like watching something she had planted come up through the ground and turn out to be a different plant from the one on the packet.

And she thought, sitting on the floor, about being seen.

She had told him at the board on the first day that the examiner had failed her for being right in front of witnesses. That had been true. It had not been the whole of it. The whole of it was that she had stood in front of that desk wanting, more than she had ever wanted anything, for somebody who understood to look at what she had done and say *yes, that, that's real*. Nobody had. The examiner had not understood it, or had not wanted to. The witnesses had understood it and said nothing, because it was not their exam.

Then a boy from Denvash had stood at a rope, three weeks ago, and watched her skip the middle against Brenna in front of the whole yard. He had given it back to her in the dusk afterward, exchange by exchange, flat, with nothing added. *You caught the chop and sent it with no middle. Three times. She went after it every time.* He had not said it was good. He had not needed to. He had seen it exactly as it was, and said it exactly as it was, and that had been better than *good*.

And this morning he had seen the other thing, the bad thing, the thing that had cost her a bout and all the time since of not knowing. He had seen it just as exactly.

It was the same eyes. That was what she kept coming back to, on the floor. The eyes that saw the skipped middle were the eyes that saw the right foot. You could not have one without the other. You could only ask the person who had them not to keep half of what they saw in a pocket.

She would ask him. Tomorrow. Not like this morning, with her voice going up and the bread whole on the post and *I'll see you*. She would ask him properly. She would tell him the rule, plainly, the way she told him everything, and let him pick it up, and it would be heavier than what other people gave him, and it would be the right weight.

And then she would show him the door.

She got up off the floor. She went to the window and stood under the sloping ceiling without hitting her head, and looked down into the lane. A dog was asleep on a step. A woman was hanging a sheet out of a window opposite. Two streets away, over the roofs, she could see a corner of Torvin's chimney with the smoke going straight up, and she found that she had chosen this room partly for that. She had not known it until now. She stood at her own window, in her own room, with her own door shut behind her, and let herself be proud, all the way through, without anybody there to see it.

Then she went down the stair, and the fourth step talked, and she said *I know* to it out loud. She went to her sparring hour across the district very late, and was shouted at, and did not mind at all.

---

He did not know whether she would be there.

He went up to the yard in the dark on Thursday morning anyway, the way he always did, by the lane where the ground began to rise, with two loaves from underneath in his arms. He told himself that it was what he did, and that Hesk had said not to change what he did. That was true. It was not why he went.

The gate in the wall was open. The frost was white along the foot of the east wall. In the grey half, at the post, somebody was striking the canvas, low, twice, and stepping round it, and striking again.

He stopped in the gateway.

Lira did not stop. She struck the post twice more and stepped round it, and he understood from the strikes that she was thinking, and that the strikes were where she had put her hands while she did it. Then she stopped, with her palm flat on the canvas, and looked at him over her shoulder.

"You're late," she said.

"I'm not."

"You're standing in the gate. That's late." She took her hand off the post. "Come here."

He came across the yard. She did not take the bread. She stood in front of him in the grey half with her hands loose at her sides and her weight a little forward, and she looked at him the way she looked at a bout between exchanges, quickly and completely.

"Here's the rule," she said. "You see a thing about me, you tell me. When you see it. Not when you're sure. Not when you've decided I'm ready. Not after Sunday. When you see it." She did not raise her voice. "My feet. My fighting. Anything. If it's wrong, it's wrong, and I'll tell you it's wrong, and we'll both have learned something. But you don't keep it. Not my things. I'll put up with you keeping your own things as long as you like. I've told you that. I'm still not asking. But mine are mine."

"All right."

"Say it back."

He said it back. He said it in his own words, the way he had said Vell's terms on the first morning, slowly, checking each one before he set it down. If he saw a thing about her, he told her when he saw it. Not when he was sure. Not when he had decided anything. He did not keep her things.

She listened to every word. When he had finished she nodded once.

"Good," she said. "That's done, then." She took one of the loaves off him at last and tore it and gave him the larger half, and the morning brightness came up in her face, slowly, as if it had been waiting behind a door. "It was still the best thing you've ever given me. I meant that too."

"I know."

"I'm going to fix it. Tired or not." She bit into the bread. "I've already started. I did the step at the post twenty times before you came, at the end of an hour, and I felt it go every time. Every time, Cael. I can *feel* it now. All that time—" She stopped, and shook her head, and went on more quietly. "A long time I couldn't find it. And now I can feel it every time."

He did not say anything. He did not think there was anything to say that would be the right size. He ate his bread.

Then she said, not looking at him, in the voice she used when a thing mattered more than she wanted it to look, "I took a room."

---

It was two streets from Torvin's, at the end of a lane he had walked down perhaps twice. It was over a chandler's shop with candles hanging from the beam by their wicks, like onions, and a widow behind the counter who looked at Cael and said, "No visitors after the lamps," before he had said anything at all.

"It isn't after the lamps," said Lira. "It's the middle of the afternoon."

"I'm saying it now," said the widow, "so it's said."

The stair went up round the side, so narrow that he had to go up it sideways, and the fourth step creaked under him like an old man complaining.

"It talks," said Lira, behind him. "Every time. I'm going to name it."

At the top there was a door.

She stopped him on the top step, before he could reach for the latch. She went past him, squeezing between him and the wall, and took a key on a string out of her jacket, and let herself in, and shut the door in his face.

He stood on the top step looking at the door. It was green gone grey, four boards and a crosspiece, with a scuff at the bottom.

"Knock," said Lira's voice, from the other side.

He knocked.

There was a pause, a long one, longer than it needed to be, a pause somebody was enjoying.

Then the latch lifted, and the door opened, and Lira stood in it with one hand on the edge and the other on the frame. She looked at him as if he were somebody who had come to the wrong house.

"Yes?" she said.

He did not laugh. He nearly did. He saw her see him nearly do it, and her face went very straight indeed.

"I've come to see the room," he said.

"Have you." She considered him. Then she stood aside and held the door open. "You can come in. Mind your head. The ceiling comes down on the window side and it's not sorry about it."

He went in, and minded his head.

It was very small. He had known it would be. There was a bed with a straw mattress on it and a blanket folded square at the foot, and a shelf. On the shelf there was a tin cup, and a comb, and the canvas roll, and a little flat tin with the picture worn off its lid. There was one window with a crack across the pane and a strip of paper over the crack. There was a hook on the back of the door. Her jacket was not on it. She was still wearing it. He thought she had not taken it off since she got the key.

He stood in the middle of the room on the pale worn strip of floor and looked at all of it, one thing at a time, the way he looked at everything.

"Well?" said Lira.

"It's yours."

He had not known that was what he was going to say until it was out. She did not say anything. She shut the door behind him, very gently. It made a small sound as the latch dropped, the smallest sound, a click like a figure coming right.

"It shuts," she said.

"I heard."

"It *locks*." She showed him the key on its string. "From inside. And from outside. Both. I can be in here and lock it, or out there and lock it, and either way it's locked." She seemed to think he might not have understood. "Nobody else has a key. The widow has one, for fires. Nobody else."

"Lira," he said. "It's a good door."

She looked at him. Then she sat down on the end of the bed, quite suddenly, as if her legs had decided it for her, and looked at the door. She did not say anything for a long time, and he did not either. He stood under the sloping ceiling with his head bent, and the light came in through the cracked pane, and two streets away over the roofs, through the window, he could see the top of Torvin's chimney with its smoke going straight up.

"You can see Torvin's from here," he said.

"Can you?" said Lira. "I hadn't noticed."

---

Vell put him on Sunday's card with a Copper Rank 4 named Talis, Stone Path.

"He's young," she said, at her table on Thursday. "Younger than Dessa. A good deal less patient. Stone like hers, and strong, and he's had a good season." She turned her tea by the handle. "You'll want to watch him tonight. He's fourth on the card."

"It's a week since Sarel," said Lira, who had come up beside him. "He's still green across the back."

"I know how long it's been. I wrote it down." Vell looked at Lira. "Survivable?"

Lira looked at Cael's back, as though she could see through his coat to the colours on it.

"Survivable," she said. "If he doesn't go down on the ribs."

He watched Talis fight that night from the rope, near the barrel, with the Log open on his knee.

Talis was about eighteen, a big fair young man with a neck as wide as his head and a way of rocking on his heels between the exchanges, impatient, as if the circle were a queue he had been made to stand in. When Vell said *begin* he did not wait for anything. He went in. Cael watched the grit come up off him as it had come up off Dessa, shivering all at once over his arms and shoulders and lying down flat. Then he was a gatepost with a temper, and he walked through a Copper 3 Blade in four exchanges, and the rope cheered.

Cael wrote it all down. He wrote the shoulders, which dipped a little before the right hand came. He wrote the hands, which Talis held low. He wrote the impatience, the rocking on the heels, the way the young man's face went red when a strike did not land. He wrote *frustration — let him*, because that had been a true thing about Brenna and might be a true thing again.

He did not write anything about the ground. He did not look at the ground. He had never once in his life looked at the ground.

---

On Sunday the frost had gone off by the middle of the afternoon, and the dirt in the circle was soft on the surface and hard underneath, and Talis came over the rope as if he were late for something.

"Talis. Copper Rank 4, Stone Path. Against unrated. Vouched." The small pause. "Begin when you're ready."

The grit came up off him and lay down flat, and he came.

Cael did what he had done in the third exchange against Sarel. He moved. He did it small and quick and unplanned, with his hips loose this time, as Lira had shown him on Wednesday. He watched Talis's shoulders while he did it, because Talis was not Sarel and the watching was not too slow for him. The shoulders dipped. The right hand came. Cael was somewhere else.

But Talis was somewhere else too.

Cael had not seen him go there. One breath Talis was in front of him, and the next he was a stride to the left, with his weight already planted. The left forearm came across, heavy and grey with the grit, and hit Cael on the upper arm like a mallet on a stump. Cael went sideways and kept his feet, just, and Talis was already rocking back on his heels, impatient, waiting.

"End of the exchange," said Vell.

*First exchange,* said Cael to himself. *The shoulders dipped before the right. I read it. He didn't use the right. He went somewhere I didn't see him go, and planted, and used the left.*

In the second it happened again.

He watched the shoulders. He watched the hands. He moved without deciding. Every time, Talis was suddenly somewhere Cael had not expected, a stride off to one side, planted, as solid as if he had grown there. Every time the strike came from the place where he had planted, with all the weight of the ground behind it. Cael got out of the first one. The second caught him on the hip, a short blunt thud like a door kicked shut, and he felt it go right through him into his back, into Sarel's bruise.

*He knows where he's going to plant before I do,* Cael thought, on his mark, with his hip aching. *Of course he does. It's his foot.* He looked at Talis's shoulders and Talis's hands. *But I should see it. There should be something before it. A dip, a lean, a look. Something.*

There was nothing. He watched for it all through the third exchange, watched until his eyes ached. He got a touch on Talis's arm, short and straight from the hip, the one strike he owned. Talis's face went red, and Cael thought *good, let him*, and nothing else came of it. Talis's temper did not make his shoulders talk, or his hands. It only made him plant harder.

In the fourth, Talis planted on Cael's left, where Cael had not seen him going. The gatepost forearm came round into Cael's side under the ribs, on the left, the side Sarel had not touched. It was the strike he never saw building. He did not see it at all. He was looking at Talis's shoulders, and the shoulders were still, and then he was on the ground.

He went down on his side with his cheek against the dirt and every bit of air gone out of him at once.

"Match," said Vell.

He lay there.

He could not get up yet. He lay with his face on the cold packed earth of the circle, and breathed, or tried to, and listened to the yard shouting above him. He thought about nothing at all for a moment.

Talis walked back to his mark.

Cael felt him go.

He felt it in his cheekbone, where his face lay against the ground. It was a faint, quick buzz, very small, like the hum in a table when somebody sets a heavy glass down at the other end of it. It came up out of the dirt into the bone of his face. Then, a half beat after it, Talis's foot came down, a stride away, and planted.
